Let me understand the task. We have STLC call-by-value with locally nameless syntax, and we need to prove strong normalization for closed well-typed terms.

Key challenges:
- The language is locally nameless with `tm_bvar`, `tm_fvar`, `open_rec`, `lc_at`.
- Typing context maps free atoms to types.
- The step relation is CBV with if-then-else.
- Need to prove strong normalization.

Plan: Develop a logical relation (unary, type-indexed) on terms. For CBV STLC with if, the standard approach:

Define `SN t := strongly_normalizing t` (Acc-based or inductive). Then define logical relation:
- `R_Bool t := SN t /\ (t -->* tm_true \/ t -->* tm_false) ...` Hmm, for SN it's easier to define `R` such that:
  - `R_Bool t := SN t` (since Bool values are true/false; if we need "canonical forms" property for the fundamental theorem on if, we need: if t is in R_Bool then t reduces to true or false. Actually for the fundamental theorem's if case, we need: if t1 : Bool and t1 is in R_Bool, then t1 steps to true/false along some path; but SN gives that t1 is normalizing, so t1 reduces to a normal form which must be a value; a value of Bool type is true or false. But wait: in CBV, t1 could be a stuck term? Closed well-typed terms can't get stuck... but the logical relation argument can be structured with "SN plus reducibility to a canonical value".

Actually the standard logical relation for strong normalization of CBV STLC:

`R_Bool t := SN t` isn't quite enough for the if case; we need if t1 ∈ R_Bool then t1 is either true, false, or reduces to one. But actually we can prove the fundamental theorem for the `if` case as follows: given t1 ∈ R_Bool, i.e. SN t1. We need SN (if t1 then t2 else t3) given SN t2, SN t3. To prove this, note: by SN of t1, t1 has a normal form? Actually SN gives that all reduction sequences terminate, but to show `if t1 then t2 else t3` is SN, we need to consider all its reductions:
- ST_IfTrue: requires t1 = tm_true, and then it steps to t2, which is SN; and t2's reducts are SN.
- ST_IfFalse: similar.
- ST_If: t1 --> t1', then need SN (if t1' then t2 else t3), by induction on SN t1 (i.e., Acc or the inductive's inversion).

The issue is when t1 is a normal form that's not true/false — then `if t1 then t2 else t3` has no reduction at the head, and is SN (it's stuck). But we need to ensure that the reducts after t1 steps remain in R_Bool. With R_Bool = SN: if t1 is SN and t1 --> t1', then t1' is SN (from SN_intro's premise). Great, so the induction works! Actually the if case works with just SN:

To show SN (if t1 then t2 else t3): use the coinductive/inductive characterization. Let's define `SN := strongly_normalizing`. We want to prove `SN (tm_if t1 t2 t3)` given SN t1, SN t2, SN t3. We can do induction on SN t1? Actually strongly_normalizing is defined inductively with SN_intro : (forall t', t --> t' -> SN t') -> SN t. So to prove SN t, we apply SN_intro and show all one-step reducts SN.

So prove by induction on the SN derivation of t1? Hmm, `SN_intro` gives `(forall t', t1 --> t' -> SN t')`, but the induction principle: we can do `induction (H1 : SN t1)` and get the premise. Actually strong normalization's induction principle: to prove P t from SN t, enough to prove P t assuming for all t' with t --> t', P t'. That's not directly derived from induction on the inductive derivation unless P is monotone... Standard: `SN` as an inductive predicate is the least fixed point of `λS. {t | ∀t', t→t' → S t'}`; `SN_intro` is the constructor. Induction on the derivation of `SN t1`: the derivation is `SN_intro t1 H` where H : ∀ t', t1 --> t' -> SN t'. In the induction step, we get the IH for each t' and each SN t' derivation... Actually the induction principle for the inductive family is:

`strongly_normalizing_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop, (forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> (forall t', t --> t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t`.

Hmm wait, the IH would be on the argument `H : forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'`, and the induction hypothesis would be `forall t' (Hstep : step t t'), P t'`? Actually no. The constructor argument is a function; Coq's induction principle for `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t` — the non-uniform/recursive occurrence means Coq's induction principle may be weak. Let me think: Coq generates the induction principle for strictly positive inductives. `SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', R t t' -> SN t') -> SN t`. The occurrence `SN t'` is under a binder inside the arrow; positivity is fine. The generated induction principle: 

```
strongly_normalizing_ind :
  forall P : tm -> Prop,
    (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') ->
              (forall t' (H : step t t'), P t') -> P t) ->
    forall t, SN t -> P t
```

Hmm, actually since the recursive arg is a function returning SN, the induction hypothesis becomes `forall t' (H : step t t'), P t'` — Coq can generate that. Yes I believe it's fine.

But actually for induction along SN derivation, another standard approach: define SN as `Acc step` (well-founded accessibility). `Acc` has a nice induction principle: if for all t' with step t t', Acc step t', then Acc step t. And `Acc_intro`. Actually the relation might not be well-founded globally, but `Acc step t` is exactly strong normalization, and `Acc_ind` is powerful.

We can define our own `SN t := Acc step t` and prove equivalence with `strongly_normalizing` (the given inductive). Or simply work directly with the given inductive `strongly_normalizing`. Let me check if `induction` on `strongly_normalizing` gives the needed IH. To be safe, I can prove a "SN step" lemma:

```coq
Lemma SN_step : forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof. intros t H. induction H as [t Hall]. intros t' Hstep. apply Hall. exact Hstep. Qed.
```

Wait, applying `Hall` directly gives SN t'. That's just it. Actually no: `SN_intro t Hall : SN t`, and `Hall : forall t', step t t' -> SN t'`. So `SN_step` is just projection. Fine.

And to prove SN of something, `apply SN_intro; intros t' Hstep` and then handle each step case. That's the standard "coinduction"/induction on derivations.

For the if case with R_Bool = SN: prove `SN (if t1 then t2 else t3)` given SN t1, SN t2, SN t3. We do induction on SN t1 with motive `fun t1 => forall t2 t3, SN t2 -> SN t3 -> SN (tm_if t1 t2 t3)`. Using `induction H1` where H1 : SN t1. The IH for each t' with t1 --> t': given SN t'... hmm, wait. With the generated induction principle, we might get IH: forall t', step t1 t' -> P t' where P t' = forall t2 t3, SN t2 -> SN t3 -> SN (if t' then t2 else t3). But we also need SN t' to apply the IH? No — P t' is stated for all t' reachable, and the induction principle provides P t' for each t' with a step. Then to prove P t1 we need to show SN (if t1 then t2 else t3), i.e., for each step `if t1 ... --> u`, SN u.

Cases on step:
- ST_IfTrue: t1 = tm_true, u = t2, need SN t2. ✓.

- ST_If: u = tm_if t1' t2 t3 with t1 --> t1', need SN (if t1' t2 t3). We need P t1' which comes from IH applied to the step t1 --> t1'. Good — if the generated induction principle gives exactly that.

Hmm, but the induction principle may give IH with additional SN hypothesis: e.g., `forall t' : tm, step t1 t' -> SN t' -> P t'`? No, the recursive argument `forall t', step t t' -> SN t'` — the induction hypothesis generated for each such argument: since the argument is a function returning SN, Coq may not generate useful IH or generates `forall t' (H : step t t'), P t'`? Let me recall how Coq handles nested recursive occurrence in an argument of an inductive constructor. For `SN_intro`, constructor type: `forall (t : tm), (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> strongly_normalizing t`. The recursive occurrence is inside the type of the argument. Let me just test.

Alternatively, avoid the issue by defining my own accessibility predicate:

```coq
Definition SN (t : tm) : Prop := Acc step t.
```

Then `SN t` unfolds to `Acc step t`; `Acc_intro : (forall y, step x y -> Acc step y) -> Acc step x`; and `Acc_ind : forall P, (forall x, (forall y, step x y -> P y) -> P x) -> forall x, Acc step x -> P x`. That's clean and we can use `induction H` where H : Acc step t gives nice IH. Then prove equivalence:

```coq
Lemma SN_iff : forall t, strongly_normalizing t <-> Acc step t.
```

Forward: induction on strongly_normalizing derivation (could be tricky with the generated principle, but we can use `fix` or well-founded induction?). Alternatively, prove directly that strongly_normalizing implies SN by the recursive structure: since strongly_normalizing is an inductive predicate whose constructor requires all successors SN, the implication `SN_strongly -> Acc step` can be proven by induction on the SN derivation; the needed IH is `forall t', step t t' -> Acc step t'`, which is exactly the premise of the constructor, no induction hypothesis needed! Because the constructor's premise already gives strongly_normalizing t' for all successors. Wait no: from `SN_intro t H` where `H : forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'`, to prove `Acc step t` we need `forall t', step t t' -> Acc step t'`. If the generated induction principle gives IH `forall t', step t t' -> P t'` where P t' = Acc step t', then we're done. If not, we can prove by a different route.

Actually there's a simpler route: `strongly_normalizing` is the inductive closure; `Acc step` satisfies the same "greatest/least" property? Hmm.

Actually, the standard equivalence: `Acc R x` iff there's no infinite R-chain from x. `strongly_normalizing` (the inductive definition) is exactly the least fixed point of `F(S) = {x | forall y, R x y -> S y}`, which is `Acc R x`? Let me see: `Acc R` is also defined inductively with constructor `Acc_intro : (forall y, R x y -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x`. They have exactly the same constructor shape! `strongly_normalizing` is literally a copy of `Acc step`. So they are isomorphic and the equivalence is trivial:

```coq
Lemma SN_Acc : forall t, strongly_normalizing t <-> Acc step t.
Proof.
  intros t; split.
  - induction 1 as [t H]; constructor; intros t' Ht'; apply H with ... 
```

Hmm, again induction issue. But since the definitions are literally the same shape, we can prove `forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> Acc step t` by `fix`:

```coq
Fixpoint SN_to_Acc (t : tm) (h : strongly_normalizing t) {struct h} : Acc step t :=
  match h with
  | SN_intro t H => Acc_intro t (fun t' Hs => SN_to_Acc t' (H t' Hs))
  end.
```

This is structurally recursive on h. But `H t' Hs : strongly_normalizing t'` is not a subterm of h structurally... Coq's guard condition might reject this because the recursive call's argument `H t' Hs` is not a subterm. Indeed, nested recursion through a function argument is problematic. Hmm.

Better: since the definitions are the same shape, maybe just prove the target theorem with `strongly_normalizing` directly and use `SN_intro` as the way to prove it. The main need for induction along SN is in proving the logical relation's closure under beta (for the App case) and in the if case.

Alternative classic approach for strong normalization: use the "reducibility" logical relation where `R_T` includes `SN`. But for CBV STLC, the standard proof uses a different trick: define the logical relation with the "neutral/canonical" structure, or use Girard's reducibility candidates, and use induction on typing derivations with a context substitution lemma.

Define logical relation:
```
R T t := match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t

  end.
```
Wait, standard reducibility for CBV: a term t of arrow type is reducible if SN t and for all v reducible at T1, t v is reducible at T2. But t2 might not be a value. We need to show t1 t2 reducible given R T1 t2. Standard approach: show that the relation is closed under "expansion" / that if t2 is reducible then either it's a value or it steps to something reducible. Hmm, this is the classic issue: R_T must be defined as a "reducibility candidate" closed under inverse reduction. Let's define:

```
R T t := SN t /\ (forall t', t --> t' -> R T t')  (* closed under reduction *)
        /\ (match T with Ty_Bool => True | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v) end)
```

But this is a recursive definition over T; that's fine (structural on T).

Wait, but defining R with the "forall t', t --> t' -> R T t'" clause makes it coinductive-ish but structurally recursive on T so fine. Actually that clause is derivable if we define properly? Let's think: standard "reducibility candidates" for CBV use:

- CR1: R T t -> SN t.
- CR2: R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
- CR3: neutral terms (stuck) whose reducts are in R are in R.

For STLC CBV, the logical relation:
- R_Bool t := SN t (plus maybe reduction closure).

Actually, here's a neat trick to avoid the self-referential same-type occurrence: define R_T as the *intersection* of SN and the "hereditary" property defined by recursion, where reduction closure at arrow type is proven as a lemma, not part of the definition. Specifically:

Define:
```
R T t := SN t /\ match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v)
  end.
```

```coq

  fix F (x : X) (a : Acc R x) {struct a} : P x :=
    match a with Acc_intro H => fun ... => F y (H y ...) end.
```

```

Also, an entirely different and often much simpler approach for strong normalization in Coq: use `Acc` and the fact that the relation `step` on well-typed terms is... Define the logical relation *using Acc*:

```
Definition SN (t : tm) := Acc step t.
```

and prove the final theorem by showing `Acc step t` for well-typed closed t, then convert `Acc step t -> strongly_normalizing t` at the end. The conversion can be done via a Fixpoint by well-founded recursion on the Acc derivation using `Acc_inv`:

```coq
Lemma Acc_SN : forall t, Acc step t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t H. induction H as [t H IH].
  apply SN_intro. intros t' Hstep.
  apply IH. exact Hstep.
Qed.
```

Wait! `induction H` where H : Acc step t. The induction principle for Acc is `Acc_ind : forall P, (forall x, (forall y, R x y -> P y) -> P x) -> forall x, Acc R x -> P x`. So `induction H as [t H IH]` gives IH : forall y, step t y -> P y where P y = strongly_normalizing y. Then apply SN_intro, intros t' Hstep, apply IH, exact Hstep. That's clean and works! `Acc_ind` exists.

So we can define SN := Acc step and at the end convert Acc to strongly_normalizing via `Acc_ind`. Actually, even simpler: prove the theorem with motive P t := strongly_normalizing t using Acc_ind directly? The logical relation will want SN := Acc step for its nice induction, then convert.

Definition SN (t : tm) := Acc full_step t.

Question 1: Does `R_SN : R T t -> SN t` hold without the SN conjunct at arrow? For arrow, if we include SN, trivial. We include it.

OK here's another standard approach: define R with three conditions explicitly (CR1-CR3 style), where the same-type reduction closure is handled via a *separate* pre-order/closure operator:

- `R_Bool t := SN t`.
- `R_Arrow T1 T2 t := SN t ∧ ∀ v, value v → R T1 v → R T2 (t v)`.

Let me look at this from another angle: maybe there's a smarter formulation of the logical relation for CBV SN that avoids inverse-reduction reasoning. 

Known result: For CBV STLC, strong normalization can be proven by interpreting types as **"hereditarily strongly normalizing"** sets defined via a "value" predicate and a "computation" predicate:

- `V_Bool = { true, false }` (values at Bool).
- `V_{T1->T2} = { abs T t | forall v ∈ V_T1, (open t v) ∈ C_T2 }`.
- `C_T = { t | SN t ∧ forall t', t -->* t' -> value t' -> t' ∈ V_T }`? 

This is the standard "value/computation" logical relation for CBV (used in e.g. step-indexed logical relations and in semantic type soundness). With this definition:
- C_T ⊆ SN.
- Closure properties: if t ∈ C_T and t --> t', then t' ∈ C_T (forward reduction closure). ✓ easy, because multi-step closure.
- Values: V_T ⊆ C_T? A value v ∈ V_T: SN v (values are SN), and any t' with v -->* t' and value t' — need t' ∈ V_T. Since v is a value that doesn't step, v -->* t' implies t' = v. So v ∈ V_T. ✓
- Fundamental theorem at arrow: abs T t ∈ V arrow requires for all v ∈ V_T1, open t v ∈ C_T2. We get it by IH. And then abs ∈ C arrow via V ⊆ C. ✓
- App case: t1 ∈ C (T1->T2), t2 ∈ C T1. Need t1 t2 ∈ C T2. C T2 requires SN (t1 t2) and: for all u, t1 t2 -->* u -> value u -> u ∈ V T2. Hmm, if the many-step reduction reaches a value u, then... the value u must be an abstraction? Not necessarily: T2 could be Bool and u = true. We need u ∈ V Bool trivial. Hmm, this direction seems hard: analyzing multi-step reductions of t1 t2.

But there's a known simpler variant for SN: define C_T := SN ∧ closure under inverse reduction? The inverse-reduction closure is exactly what makes the App case work:

`C T t := SN t ∧ (forall t', t --> t' -> C T t') ∧ (match T with Bool => True | Arrow T1 T2 => forall v, value v -> V T1 v -> C T2 (t v) end)`.

But the "forall t', t --> t' -> C T t'" is a negative occurrence of C at the same type — needs coinduction or a different encoding.

Hmm hold on. Let's reconsider: maybe a **type-directed SN measure** approach could work without logical relations? For CBV STLC, can we prove SN by a direct induction on typing derivations with a suitable notion? That's essentially what logical relations do.

Let me define (for full_step, call-by-name-ish, no F_Abs):

```
Definition SN t := Acc full_step t.

Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Properties to prove:
(P1) R T t -> SN t. (immediate, for arrow by conjunction).
(P2) R T t -> full_step t t' -> R T t'. (proved above by induction on T, using F_App1.)
(P3) Expansion at application: `R T t' -> full_step t t' -> ???` — not true generally, but for specific forms.

Wait, maybe (P2) can be inverted: Given R T (app (abs T1 t1) u), can we derive R T (open t1 u)? Not needed.

Let's revisit the fundamental theorem and see exactly what's needed, then try to prove those specific expansion lemmas.

Fundamental theorem statement (with full_step semantics):
```
forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)) -> R T (subst_env sigma t)
```
The key cases:
- T_Var: hypothesis directly.
- T_Abs: need R (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst_env sigma t1)), i.e., SN (that) ∧ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u) where body = subst_env sigma t1.
  - SN abs: trivially (Acc_intro with no reducts; since without F_Abs, abs has no reducts).
  - For u: need R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u). We know from IH on the premise with environment sigma' = x |-> u; sigma (for fresh x): R T2 (subst_env sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))). If we can show subst_env sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open body u. So the lemma generalizes to open_rec k with (fvar x) and u: 
      `subst_env (update sigma x u) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst_env sigma t)` under x not free in t.
      Note the binding depth k doesn't matter for fvar substitution since fvars aren't affected by depth. Good.
    - app, true, false, if: structural. ✓.
  
  So we get the equality: `subst_env sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst_env sigma t1) u`.
  
  So R T2 (open body u) holds.
  Then need R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u) from R T2 (open body u). This is the expansion lemma. Note here the "abs" is a specific value. Let me call this `R_beta_expand`.

- T_App: need R T2 (app (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2)) from R (T1->T2) (subst_env sigma t1) and R T1 (subst_env sigma t2). So `R_App` is immediate from the definition: R (T1->T2) u1 = SN u1 ∧ forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (u1 u). Apply to u2. ✓✓.

Now, does step ⊆ full_step hold? Let's define full_step to include exactly the congruence rules needed:
```
Inductive full_step : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  | F_beta : forall T t u, full_step (tm_app (tm_abs T t) u) (open t u)
  | F_app1 : forall t1 t1' t2, full_step t1 t1' -> full_step (tm_app t1 t2) (tm_app t1' t2)
  | F_app2 : forall t1 t2 t2', full_step t2 t2' -> full_step (tm_app t1 t2) (tm_app t1 t2')
  | F_if_true : forall t1 t2, full_step (tm_if tm_true t1 t2) t1
  | F_if_false : forall t1 t2, full_step (tm_if tm_false t1 t2) t2
  | F_if : forall t1 t1' t2 t3, full_step t1 t1' -> full_step (tm_if t1 t2 t3) (tm_if t1' t2 t3).
```
Then every `step t t'` maps to `full_step t t'`:
- ST_AppAbs: F_beta with u = v. (No need lc or value.)
- ST_App1: F_app1. ST_App2: F_app2. ST_IfTrue/False: F_if_true/false. ST_If: F_if.
Yes.

Then: if `Acc full_step t` and we have a `step`-reduction sequence, it's a `full_step`-reduction sequence, so `Acc step t` follows. Specifically, we can prove: `Acc full_step t -> Acc step t` by `Acc_ind` on Acc full_step? Let's see: P t := Acc step t. Given Acc full_step t, Acc_ind gives: for all t, (forall y, full_step t y -> P y) -> P t. To build Acc step t, use Acc_intro: for all t', step t t' -> Acc step t'. We have step t t' -> full_step t t' -> P t' (by IH). So `Acc_full_step_to_step : forall t, Acc full_step t -> Acc step t`:
```
intros t H. induction H as [t H IH]. apply Acc_intro. intros t' Hs. apply IH. apply full_of_step. exact Hs.
```
Wait, `induction H` with H : Acc full_step t using Acc_ind gives IH : forall y, full_step t y -> Acc step y. Then Acc_intro step t requires forall t', step t t' -> Acc step t'. Given step t t', we have full_step t t', so apply IH. ✓. Nice.

Then finally `strongly_normalizing t` from `Acc step t`:
```
intros t H. induction H as [t H IH]. apply SN_intro. intros t' Hs. apply IH. exact Hs.
```
Wait, `Acc step t` unfolding: `induction H` with H : Acc step t gives IH : forall y, step t y -> strongly_normalizing y, and goal strongly_normalizing t. Apply SN_intro with premise forall t', step t t' -> SN t'; use IH. ✓.

Great, this approach avoids direct reasoning about `step`'s lc conditions entirely! The lc conditions are just dropped in full_step. Very nice.

Idea: define R_T by recursion on T with an explicit closure under inverse full_step at the same type, but implement the same-type recursion using `SN` (Acc) induction so the definition is well-founded. For instance:

There's a known technique: define `R T` in a **negative/impredicative** style where the closure under reduction is baked in:

```
Definition R T := 
  match T with
  | Bool => SN
  | Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => forall t', t -->* t' -> 
       (SN t') /\ (forall v value, R T1 v -> R T2 (t' v))
  end.
```

Hmm, the "forall t', t -->* t'" multi-step reduces the dependence to t' being reached. But R T1/R T2 are structurally smaller so recursion is fine. The arrow case now is closed under reduction by construction: if t --> t', then for t'' with t' -->* t'', t -->* t'' through t', so properties transfer. And beta expansion: for `app (abs) u`, need to show for all w with app (abs) u -->* w: SN w ∧ (∀ v ∈ R T1, R T2 (w v)). Given a multi-step sequence from app (abs) u: the first step is either beta (w' = open t u, then w reached by further steps) or F_app2 (u --> u1, then from app (abs) u1). Hmm, and multi-step sequences: after beta, open t u -->* w. We know R T2 (open t u), but its closure property (as defined in the new R) doesn't directly give R T2 w for multi-step successors? Hmm. Let's think about whether there's a fundamentally simpler relation for CBV SN.

Known approach from the literature for CBV SN (e.g., "Normalization by evaluation" or Girard's "Proofs and Types" for CBV?): One uses a **Kripke-style logical relation over a "value" relation and "computation" relation**, where:
- V_Bool = {true,false}.
- V_{A->B} = { λx.M | ∀V ∈ V_A, M[V/x] ∈ C_B }.
- C_T = { M | M ∈ SN ∧ ∀ M'. M →* M' ∧ value M' → M' ∈ V_T }.

This is the standard "computational" LR. Let's check the fundamental theorem with this:

Fundamental theorem: for closed-ish terms with environments mapping fvars to values in V_T? Hmm, environments map fvars to values. Let's define semantic typing `Gamma |= t ∈ T` as: for all value environments ρ with ρ(x) ∈ V_{Gamma(x)}, subst ρ t ∈ C_T. Then:
- T_Abs: need abs T1 t1 ∈ C (T1->T2). (a) SN: abs is SN. (b) for any M' with abs -->* M' and value M', M' = abs, need abs ∈ V (T1->T2): for all v ∈ V T1, open t1 v ∈ C T2. By IH with environment extended by v (a value!), the premise gives `open t1 (fvar x)` semantically typed with context x|->T1; substituting ρ' = x|->v gives open (subst ρ t1) v ∈ C T2. ✓. And we need the fvar x to be fresh... the premise holds for fresh x, and substitution lemma gives the equality. ✓.
- T_App: u1 ∈ C (T1->T2), u2 ∈ C T1. Need app u1 u2 ∈ C T2.
  (a) SN (app u1 u2): This is the hard part but standard: since u1 is SN and u2 is SN, the application is SN? Hmm, for CBV, app u1 u2 reduces via app1 (if u2 lc) or app2 (if u1 is a value), or beta. Since u1 ∈ C hence SN, u2 ∈ C hence SN. Is app of two SN terms SN? In general no (e.g., u1 = Y combinator) but for well-typed... In the LR setting, we need to prove it. Standard approach: induct on SN u1 and SN u2. If u1 is a value: then u1 ∈ V? Not necessarily — values of arrow type are abstractions in this language (no other value formers at arrow type, since values are abs/true/false and only abs has arrow type... syntactically, values: abs, true, false. If T1->T2 and u1 is a value, it could be true/false — but those are Bool-typed, so in a well-typed setting they can't be arrow-typed. But in the LR, V_{A->B} is defined as a subset of values and we can define it as {abs T t | ...} — so a value u1 ∈ C arrow with u1 = true would satisfy C's second condition only if true ∈ V arrow, which is false since V arrow only contains abstractions. But does C's second condition force value terms to be in V? C_T = {M | SN M ∧ ∀ M', M →* M' → value M' → M' ∈ V_T}. Applying to M'=M when M is a value gives M ∈ V_T. So if true ∈ C arrow, then true ∈ V arrow, contradiction — hence true ∉ C arrow. Good, but our C is defined in terms of V, so for the App case with u1 ∈ C arrow and u1 a value, we get u1 ∈ V arrow, and V arrow = {abs T t | ∀v ∈ V T1, open t v ∈ C T2}. Not obviously; `open t u2` doesn't reduce when u2 reduces (no congruence under substitution). So this approach also has the same issue.

Wait, how does the standard CBV logical relation prove strong normalization then? Let me recall: In the "value/computation" split, the App case is usually done by induction on the multi-step evaluation of u1 to a value and u2 to a value, using the fact that closed well-typed terms **evaluate to values** (progress + preservation), not just SN. Let me recall the actual standard proof of strong normalization for CBV STLC. I remember it being done with a **logical relation using "SN plus closure under inverse reduction"**, where the arrow relation is defined as a *greatest fixed point*-like via accessibility. Or one uses **Tait's method with "reducibility" defined as SN plus a "hereditary" condition**, and proves closure under expansion using **induction on the type and the SN of the argument**.

Let me look at this from a different angle: perhaps we can define R in terms of *strong normalization of all applications*, in a way that's inherently closed under expansion:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => SN t /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
and the key expansion lemma to prove is precisely E(T2). Let me try to prove E by induction on T2 and see what's needed, being very careful. Maybe it actually goes through by a nested induction on SN u and the structure of T2, without needing anything extra, because the arrow case's application to v can use E at the *component* types with suitable terms.

E(T2): Given H : forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open t u'), and Hu : R T1 u, prove R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u).

Induction on T2:
Case Bool: as above, Acc induction on SN u (which follows from R T1 u via P1). Reducts of app (abs) u:
  - beta: open t u. H u Hu. ✓ (R T2 = SN here)

Then for app2 reduct u' with u --> u': apply IH u' step. We need R T1 u' (P2) and H (fixed). ✓.

  (b) forall v, R A v -> R B (app (app (abs T1 t) u) v).

      Need: R B (app (app (abs T1 t) u) v).

Now, how to prove R B (app (app (abs) u) v) from R B (app (open t u) v)? The term `app (app (abs) u) v` reduces:
  - F_app1 (inner): inner app (abs) u --> open t u, so outer --> app (open t u) v. That's in R B (given).
  - F_app1/F_app2 (inner): inner app (abs) u --> app (abs) u' for u --> u', so outer --> app (app (abs) u') v.
  - F_app2 (outer): v --> v', so outer --> app (app (abs) u) v'.
So we can do a *mutual* Acc induction on SN u and SN v to show R B (app (app (abs) u) v), using the analogous hypothesis at (u', v) [which requires H at u' and R A v] and at (u, v') [which requires R A v' and Hab' = R B (app (open t u) v')].

Note that for the (u, v') case, we need R B (app (open t u) v'), which follows from H u Hu's second conjunct applied to v' (R A v' from P2 and SN v). ✓. And for the (u', v) case, we need the expansion hypothesis at u': H works for all u' with R T1 u' — H is universally quantified over u', and R T1 u' comes from P2. ✓. So we need a *generalized* lemma E':

E'(u, v): Given U : forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open t u') and Hu : R T1 u and Hv : R A v (when T2 = A -> B), prove R B (app (app (abs T1 t) u) v).

General Lemma (K): For all T, and terms s, if:
  (i) SN s?

`Exp T s := for all ...`? 

Let's define a "contextual" relation: R T s is defined as:
```
R T s := SN s /\ forall s', s -->* s' -> match T with
   | Bool => True

   end
```
Wait, this multi-step version could systematically solve the expansion issue! Let's think again:

Define (call it Rh for "hereditary"):
```
Fixpoint Rh (T : ty) (s : tm) : Prop :=
  SN s /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall s', s -->* s' -> forall v, value v -> Rh A v -> Rh B (tm_app s' v)
  end.
```
(Using value v in the arrow property; we could also use arbitrary, but values might suffice since applications in CBV beta happen to values.)

Now:
- Rh_SN: Rh T s -> SN s (first conjunct).
- Rh_step: Rh T s -> s --> s' -> Rh T s'. Proof: SN s' from Acc_inv; arrow case: for s'' with s' -->* s'', we have s -->* s'' (multi_step with s --> s'), so use the original property. ✓✓. This is where the multi-step formulation shines — forward closure is trivial!
- Rh_value: is every value in Rh? Need SN and arrow property. For a value abs T1 t: arrow property: for s' with abs -->* s', s' = abs (values don't step), then for v ∈ Rh A (value v), need Rh B (abs v). Hmm, this is again the beta-expansion issue but now at multi-step level. Hmm.

- Expansion lemma: Rh T (open t u) -> ...? We need Rh T (app (abs) u) given Rh T (open t u) and Rh T1 u (and maybe value u?). With the multi-step definition, let's prove:
  Lemma Rh_beta: forall T1 T2 t u,
    (forall u', Rh T1 u' -> Rh T2 (open t u')) ->
    Rh T1 u -> Rh T2 (app (abs T1 t) u).

Note: the Acc induction motive needs to be exactly Rh T2 (app (abs) u), so we need the full conclusion, including the arrow property, to be established inductively. Let's see.
  - Arrow property (T2 = Arrow A B): For all s' with app (abs) u -->* s', and all value v with Rh A v: Rh B (s' v).
    We need to analyze the multi-step sequence from app (abs) u.

    Case the sequence starts with F_app2: app (abs) u --> app (abs) u1 -->* s' where u --> u1. Then s' is reached from app (abs) u1 by a shorter multi-step. By Acc IH on SN u with u1 (SN from Rh_step), we get Rh T2 (app (abs) u1), whose arrow property gives Rh B (s' v). ✓.
    So the refl case remains: need Rh B (app (app (abs) u) v) given Rh B (app (open t u) v) (which comes from H u Hu's arrow property at refl and value v).

      - app (open t u) v (via F_app1 on the inner beta) — in Rh B, given.

Wait, but we need Rh B (app (app (abs) u) v'), and we'd want to prove it by induction on SN v using the hypothesis at v'?         * beta at the outer? No, the outer function app (abs) u is not an abs.

      So this requires a **double Acc induction on u and v** to establish Rh B (app (app (abs) u) v). But wait — our outer induction was already an Acc induction on u for the purpose of proving Rh T2 (app (abs) u). Hmm. The fundamental difficulty: Rh arrow's property is restricted to value arguments, but R terms can be non-values.

Let me reconsider: maybe restrict R to *values* at arrow type? I.e., define R such that non-value terms get in via reduction to values. Since the language is deterministic-ish? CBV is deterministic except for if with a non-value condition? Actually in CBV, the reduction relation here is not fully deterministic because ST_App1 (reduce function) and ST_App2 (reduce argument when function is a value) are mutually exclusive by value-ness, and beta... The relation is deterministic in the sense that at most one rule applies? Let's check: `app t1 t2`: rules ST_AppAbs (t1 = abs, v = t2 value), ST_App1 (t1 steps), ST_App2 (t1 value, t2 steps). If t1 = abs and t2 value: ST_AppAbs applies; ST_App1 doesn't (abs doesn't step); ST_App2 doesn't (t2 doesn't step). If t1 = abs and t2 not value and steps: ST_App2 applies; ST_AppAbs doesn't (v not value); ST_App1 doesn't. If t1 not value and steps: ST_App1; ST_AppAbs no; ST_App2 requires value t1 no. If t1 not value and doesn't step: stuck. So yes, step is deterministic (single reduct at most). Similarly `if t1 ...`: ST_IfTrue/False when t1 = true/false (and those don't step), ST_If when t1 steps; deterministic. So `step` is a partial function: at most one reduct.

This is important! With determinism, inverse-reduction closure is much easier: t has at most one reduct. Then the logical relation can be defined as: t ∈ R T iff SN t and all reducts in R T and (canonical form conditions). And for the expansion lemma, app (abs) u has at most one reduct: open t u (if... wait, no! app (abs) u with u arbitrary: ST_AppAbs requires value u and lc (abs); ST_App2 applies if u steps (then value (abs) is true? v_abs requires lc abs yes, so ST_App2 applies). So if u is a value: reduct = open t u. If u is not a value but steps to u': reduct = app (abs) u'. If u is not a value and stuck: no reduct. Deterministic. ✓.

Hmm, but our full_step is NOT deterministic (F_app1 and F_app2 both can apply to app (abs) u: F_app1 needs abs to step — no (no F_Abs); F_app2 yes if u steps; beta yes regardless of u. So for app (abs) u where u steps: full_step has TWO reducts: open t u (beta) and app (abs) u' (F_app2). That's why SN is harder for full_step. But SN for full_step implies SN for step; and we chose full_step to make congruence easy. But the non-determinism complicates the logical relation.

Maybe instead prove SN for `step` directly with a logical relation, exploiting determinism! Since step is deterministic, the relation can be defined in terms of "all reducts", and inverse-step closure holds for terms with a unique reduct.

Hmm, the side conditions (lc) still bite.

Wait, in CBV, `app t1 t2` steps by:
- ST_AppAbs if t1 = abs and t2 is a value.
- ST_App1 if t1 --> t1' AND locally_closed t2.

**Tait's method for CBV** (from e.g. "Reducibility and PER" or Harper): Define:
- `SN` = strong normalization.
- `R_Bool t := SN t`.

- Then prove a "value lemma": if `v` is a value, `v ∈ V_A ↔ v ∈ R_A` (since values are SN and any value in R arrow... hmm).
- The key lemma: if `t ∈ R_A` then either `t` is a value in `V_A` or it steps to something in `R_A`. Since SN, if t is not a value, t has some reduct; but is that reduct in R_A? Not necessarily from SN alone. Hmm.

Let's step back and think about how to prove the beta-expansion lemma with the multi-step Rh definition, but perhaps we can **strengthen the definition of Rh at arrow types to quantify over all terms in Rh A, not just values**, and include the application to arbitrary terms as part of the relation itself:

```
Fixpoint Rh (T : ty) (s : tm) : Prop :=
  SN s /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall s', s -->* s' -> forall u, Rh A u -> Rh B (tm_app s' u)
  end.
```

Now the arrow property applies to arbitrary u ∈ Rh A. This makes the App case *immediate*: Rh (A->B) u1 and Rh A u2 gives Rh B (app u1 u2) using s' = u1 (refl). 

Now the beta expansion lemma: given H : forall u', Rh T1 u' -> Rh T2 (open t u') — wait, now for the T_Abs case under the fundamental theorem with environment, the IH gives exactly this for all u' (since environment extends with u' and Rh T1 u'). - SN part: as before via Acc induction on SN u. ✓ (using H at u and at successors).
- Arrow part (T2 = A->B): forall s' with app (abs) u -->* s', forall w ∈ Rh A, Rh B (s' w).
  Analyze the sequence s':
  - refl: s' = app (abs) u. Need Rh B (app (app (abs) u) w). Hmm, same refl issue!
  - beta-first: open t u -->* s'. H u Hu : Rh (A->B) (open t u) = SN ∧ forall s'' with open -->* s'', forall w ∈ Rh A, Rh B (s'' w). Apply. ✓.
  - F_app2-first: app (abs) u1 -->* s' with u --> u1. Use Acc IH (Rh T2 (app (abs) u1)). ✓.

  However! Observe that we can prove a generalized lemma by induction on the *number of arguments* or by proving a lemma of the form:

  Lemma Rh_abs_apps: forall T1 T2 t u (H : forall u', Rh T1 u' -> Rh T2 (open t u')), Rh T1 u -> Rh T2 (app (abs T1 t) u).
  
  and in the refl arrow case, we need Rh B (app (app (abs) u) w). We could prove this as a separate induction **on w's SN** combined with **on u's SN**:
  
  Sub-lemma (S): forall u w, H -> Rh T1 u -> Rh A w -> Rh B (app (app (abs T1 t) u) w).
  Prove by double Acc induction on u and w. Requirements for Rh B (app (app (abs) u) w):
    - SN: reducts:
       (i) app (open t u) w (via F_app1 on the beta step) — given by H u Hu's arrow property at refl with w. ✓
       (ii) app (app (abs) u1) w for u --> u1 — Sub-lemma at (u1, w) via Acc IH on u. ✓
       (iii) app (app (abs) u) w1 for w --> w1 — Sub-lemma at (u, w1) via Acc IH on w, and Rh A w1 by Rh_step. ✓

       (ii) inner app2: app (app (abs) u1) w -->* s': use Sub-lemma at (u1, w), its arrow property. ✓
       (iii) outer app2: app (app (abs) u) w1 -->* s': use Sub-lemma at (u, w1). ✓
       (iv) refl: s' = app (app (abs) u) w. Need Rh D (app (app (app (abs) u) w) z). This is now an application with three arguments... and we're back to the same issue one level deeper!

So this "hereditary application" structure is inherently infinite — exactly why the relation is defined by recursion on the type: the arrow property should only quantify over terms at the *argument type*, and the "refl" case reappears at the next type B, requiring Rh B of the applied term — which is what we're proving... Wait, in the Sub-lemma for Rh B (app (app (abs) u) w) with B = C->D, the refl case needs Rh D (app (app (app (abs) u) w) z). Could we get this from Sub-lemma at a *different* type? Sub-lemma is about Rh B' (app (app (abs T1 t) u) w) for the SAME abstraction abs T1 t and arguments u, w — but here we have three arguments, and the abstraction is still abs T1 t. The type B has changed to D; the term is app (app (app (abs T1 t) u) w) z. Hmm, the "abs T1 t" expects one argument u; the extra w, z are applications of the result.

Observe: the term app (app (abs T1 t) u) w = app f w where f = app (abs T1 t) u. And H gives that the *beta reduct* term f' = app (open t u) is in Rh (A->B) (as `open t u`), and f' w ∈ Rh B. So the question is: is `Rh B (f w)` implied by `Rh B (f' w)` when f --> f'? This is exactly the "expansion closure" property at type B for the pair of terms f w and f' w. And by the type-directed structure, maybe we can prove a general lemma:

Now let's redo the fundamental theorem with R including lc and using `step`:

R T t := locally_closed t ∧ SN t ∧ (match T with Bool => True | Arrow A B => forall u, R A u -> R B (app t u) end).

Actually, maybe we can define R as a Fixpoint:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ SN t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => True
  | Ty_Arrow A B => forall u, R A u -> R B (tm_app t u)
  end.
```
Note the occurrence of R at A and B (strict subterms) — fine.

Properties:
(P_lc) R T t -> locally_closed t: first conjunct.
(P_SN) R T t -> SN t: second conjunct.
(P_step) R T t -> step t t' -> R T t': forward closure.
  - lc t' from lc t and step (lc preservation).
  - SN t' from Acc_inv.

Hmm, but hold on: is `step` actually deterministic in the presence of the side condition `locally_closed t2` in ST_App1? Let's verify determinism carefully: For a given term, at most one rule applies. Two different rules could apply only if the term matches both shapes:

Let's not overcomplicate: in our E, the term is app (abs T1 t) u with T2 the result type. R T2 (app ...) has arrow condition forall w ∈ R A, R B (app (app (abs T1 t) u) w). The term `app (app (abs T1 t) u) w` is an app, not an abs, so K at type B with s = app (app (abs) u) w: the extra abstraction hypothesis is vacuous. When proving K's arrow condition, we recurse to K at type B with term s w where s w is an app; still vacuous. Great, so the recursion never needs the extra hypothesis except at the top level where s = app (abs) u — also an app. So the extra hypothesis is never actually used! Then why include it? Because K's proof at arrow type considers s w, which is an app, and the reduct case ST_AppAbs requires s = abs — impossible for an app. So for application-headed terms, everything works!

So let's specialize K to terms that are applications? Hmm, but in the recursion, s w is an application always. And the initial term f = app (abs) u is an application. So define:

**Lemma K_app**: For all T, terms f of the form tm_app s u (any application), if
   lc f, SN f, and forall f', step f f' -> R T f',
   then R T f.

Wait, is this true? Let's prove by induction on T:
- Bool: lc ✓, SN ✓.
- Arrow A B: need forall w ∈ R A, R B (f w). Apply IH at type B to the term f w:
   - lc (f w) ✓ (lc f, lc w).
   - SN (f w): reducts of f w:
      * ST_App1: f --> f1, reduct f1 w. Need SN (f1 w). We know R T f1 (from the hypothesis), hence by the arrow condition of R (A->B) f1: R B (f1 w), hence SN. ✓
      * ST_AppAbs: f = abs — impossible since f is an application (syntactic). ✓
      * ST_App2: value f — impossible since f is an application and values are abs/true/false. ✓
     So all reducts of f w are SN, hence SN (f w) by Acc_intro. ✓
   - all reducts of f w in R B: as above: ST_App1 reduct f1 w ∈ R B ✓; ST_AppAbs impossible; ST_App2 impossible (f not a value). ✓
   - Then IH at B gives R B (f w). ✓
   
Wow, that's clean! The key point: applications are never values, so `f w` only reduces via ST_App1 (reducing f), and the reduct handler is directly available from the hypothesis `R T f1`. And ST_App1 requires lc w, which we have from R A w. 

So now, back to E: we want R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u) given H, Hu. We can try to prove:
   lc ✓, SN ✓, and forall reducts f' of f = app (abs T1 t) u, R T2 f'. Then K_app gives R T2 f.
Reducts of f:
   - ST_AppAbs: requires value u, f' = open t u. R T2 (open t u) from H u Hu ✓.
   - ST_App1: requires abs steps — impossible.
   - ST_App2: requires value (abs T1 t) (true if Habs) and u --> u'. f' = app (abs T1 t) u'. Need R T2 f' — by Acc IH on u' (SN u' from Acc_inv), with R T1 u' (R_step Hu) and H. ✓.
So we need to prove SN f and (forall reducts R T2) by Acc induction on SN u. Actually, the SN part follows from the reducts being R T2 (each R T2 f' gives SN f' by R_SN), so Acc_intro gives SN f. But there's a chicken-and-egg: K_app requires SN f; but we can prove SN f from the reduct condition directly via Acc_intro (all reducts have SN). And the reduct condition is proven using the Acc IH on u. Let's structure:

E(T1, T2, t, u): by Acc induction on SN u:
  Given Habs, H, Hu.
  We prove both:
    (1) forall f', step (app (abs T1 t) u) f' -> R T2 f'
    (2) SN (app (abs T1 t) u)
  Reducts analysis:
    - ST_AppAbs with value u: f' = open t u; R T2 via H u Hu.
    - ST_App1: impossible (abs doesn't step).
    - ST_App2 with u --> u': f' = app (abs T1 t) u'; apply Acc IH to u' (Acc from Acc_inv) with R T1 u' (R_step Hu) → gives R T2 f'. ✓
  Then SN f via Acc_intro (all reducts SN via R_SN). Then K_app T2 f with lc f, SN f, and the reduct condition gives R T2 f. ✓✓.

This works! And K_app's proof by induction on T2 uses the *same* reduct condition at type B with the term f w, where the reduct condition is derived from R (A->B) f1's arrow part. Let me double check K_app's proof doesn't need SN of f w from something else:
  IH at B is literally: forall f (application), lc f -> SN f -> (forall f', step f f' -> R B f') -> R B f.
  To apply it to f w, we need SN (f w) and the reduct condition. We showed SN (f w) via Acc_intro with each reduct in R B (hence SN). But careful: Acc_intro for SN (f w) requires forall y, step (f w) y -> Acc y. Each such y = f1 w (ST_App1, since f is an app so f w is an app: could ST_AppAbs apply to f w? That requires f w = app (abs) — i.e., f = abs, contradiction; ST_App2 requires value f w — apps aren't values). So each reduct is f1 w with step f f1 and lc w. R T f1 by hypothesis (T = A->B), and R T f1's arrow condition gives R B (f1 w) (needs R A w ✓). Hence Acc (f1 w) via R_SN. So SN (f w) ✓ via Acc_intro. Then the reduct condition for f w (same analysis) gives R B (f1 w) ✓. Then IH_B applies. ✓✓.

Excellent. So the proof structure is:

1. Define `SN t := Acc step t` (or use strongly_normalizing? We'll use Acc).
2. Define R as Fixpoint with lc, SN, and arrow application to arbitrary R terms.
3. `R_SN`, `R_lc`.
4. `step_det : step t u -> step t v -> u = v` (determinism).
5. `step_value : value v -> ~ step v v'` (values don't step).
6. `step_abs : ~ step (abs T t) t'`.
7. `lc_step_back : step s s' -> lc s' -> lc s`.
8. `lc_step_fwd : step s s' -> lc s -> lc s'`. (Maybe needed for R_step.)
9. `R_step_fwd : R T s -> step s s' -> R T s'` (forward closure; needs lc fwd, ST_App1 congruence with lc u).
10. `R_step_inv : R T s' -> step s s' -> R T s` (inverse closure; needs determinism, lc back, ST_App1).
11. `R_stuck : lc s -> (forall s', ~ step s s') -> ~ value s -> R T s` (by induction on T).
12. `K_app : forall T f, is_app f -> lc f -> SN f -> (forall f', step f f' -> R T f') -> R T f` (by induction on T).
    Actually, we can just require `forall s u, f = tm_app s u` or a predicate is_app. Since applications are the only case, define is_app f := exists s u, f = tm_app s u. In the proof, we need "f is not a value" and "f cannot be an abs". Easier: state with explicit s u: `forall T s u, lc (tm_app s u) -> SN (tm_app s u) -> (forall f', step (tm_app s u) f' -> R T f') -> R T (tm_app s u)`. In the induction step at arrow, we consider the term `tm_app (tm_app s u) w` — which is an application (with function part `tm_app s u`). So the lemma needs to apply to arbitrary applications, and the recursive term is app (app s u) w. We can state the lemma for a general application term with pattern (tm_app a b).

    Statement: `K_app : forall T a b, lc (tm_app a b) -> SN (tm_app a b) -> (forall f', step (tm_app a b) f' -> R T f') -> R T (tm_app a b)`.
    Proof by induction on T:
    - Bool: R = lc ∧ SN ✓.
    - Arrow A B: R = lc ∧ SN ∧ (forall w, R A w -> R B (app (app a b) w)).
      Need for the last: apply IH_B to the term `tm_app (tm_app a b) w` (which is of the form tm_app a' b' with a' = tm_app a b, b' = w):
        * lc ✓.
        * SN: as analyzed, reduce to the reduct condition. But maybe we can get SN directly: any reduct of app (app a b) w is (app a' w) where a -->... wait, the function part is `tm_app a b` (an app). ST_App1 requires step (tm_app a b) f1 and lc w: reduct tm_app f1 w. We have R T f1 (hypothesis) → R B (app f1 w) ✓ (arrow condition) → SN. ST_AppAbs impossible (function is an app, not abs). ST_App2 impossible (app not value). So SN via Acc_intro ✓.
        * reduct condition: same analysis ✓.
        * Apply IH_B. ✓.
      Note that IH_B is from the induction on T (T = A -> B, B is a strict subterm). ✓.

13. E: `R_beta : forall T1 T2 t u, R T1 u -> (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open t u')) -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u)`.
    Proof: Acc induction on SN u (from R_SN Hu). Given Habs : locally_closed (tm_abs T1 t) — hmm, we need this for ST_AppAbs. Where does it come from?     The proof: Acc induction on u:
      IH: forall u', step u u' -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u') (given H, R T1 u').
      Goal R T2 (app (abs) u).
      We prove `Hall : forall f', step (app (abs) u) f' -> R T2 f'` by inversion:
        - ST_AppAbs: f' = open t u; H u Hu ✓.
        - ST_App1: step (abs T1 t) ??? — impossible by step_abs.
        - ST_App2: value (abs T1 t) [from Habs], step u u'; f' = app (abs) u'; apply IH. Need R T1 u' from R_step_fwd Hu. ✓
      Then SN (app (abs) u) via Acc_intro + Hall + R_SN.
      Then K_app with lc (app) ✓, SN ✓, Hall ✓. Done.
    Note: the Acc induction principle: `induction Hacc as [u Hstep IH]` gives IH : forall u', step u u' -> P u', where P u' is `R T1 u' -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u')`? Hmm, we need H and Habs fixed. Since H and Habs are parameters, the motive should include `R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u)`. Let's set it up:
    ```
    Lemma R_beta : forall T1 T2 t u, locally_closed (tm_abs T1 t) -> R T1 u -> (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open t u')) -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u).
    Proof.
      intros T1 T2 t u Habs Hu H.
      revert Hu.
      remember (something) ... 
    ```
    To do Acc induction on `R_SN Hu`, we can do:
    ```
    assert (Hacc : Acc step u) by (apply R_SN in Hu; exact Hu).
    revert Hu. 
    induction Hacc as [u Hstep IH]; intros Hu.
    ```
    Hmm, `induction Hacc` where Hacc : Acc step u — but Hacc depends on Hu? No, it's a separate assertion. `assert (Hacc : Acc step u) by exact (R_SN T1 u Hu)`. Then `revert Hu; induction Hacc as [u Hstep IH].` Wait, if we revert Hu, then the goal is `R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u)`, and `induction Hacc` with Hacc : Acc step u works if u is still a variable... Actually after `revert Hu`, u is in the context with Hacc. `induction Hacc as [u Hstep IH]` will generalize u? 14. Fundamental theorem:
```
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, (forall x S, Gamma x = Some S -> R S (sigma x)) -> R T (subst_env sigma t).
```
Wait, we also might need a condition ensuring lc of sigma images? R S (sigma x) includes lc ✓.

Induction on has_type derivation.
- T_Var: H0 : Gamma x = Some T; env gives R T (sigma x); subst_env sigma (fvar x) = sigma x. ✓
- T_True/T_False: R Bool (subst_env sigma true) = R Bool true = lc ∧ SN. lc true ✓; SN true ✓ (no reducts).
- T_Abs: Gamma, T1, T2, t1, L; premise Hpre : forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (x|->T1;Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2. IH for the premise: forall x Hx, forall sigma, env_ok (x|->T1;Gamma) sigma -> R T2 (subst_env sigma (open t1 (fvar x))).
  Goal: R (T1->T2) (subst_env sigma (tm_abs T1 t1)) = R (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst_env sigma t1)).
  Need:
    * lc (tm_abs T1 body): where body = subst_env sigma t1.
    * SN (tm_abs T1 body): abs doesn't step → Acc_intro vacuous (need step_abs).
    * forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 body) u).
  Pick a fresh x: x not in L and x not free in t1 (and maybe x not free in... sigma? sigma is fixed; we need x ∉ FV(t1)). Since atoms are nat and L/fv finite, choose fresh x.
  Let sigma' := update sigma x u.
  From IH: R T2 (subst_env sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))) — wait, the premise Hpre needs x with ~ In x L; and env_ok (x|->T1;Gamma) sigma': 
     - For y = x: (x|->T1;Gamma) x = Some T1, need R T1 (sigma' x) = R T1 u ✓.
     - For y ≠ x: (x|->T1;Gamma) y = Gamma y, need R (Gamma y) (sigma' y) = R (Gamma y) (sigma y) ✓ from env.
  Then substitution lemma: subst_env sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst_env sigma t1) u, given x not free in t1.
  So H : forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open body u') — holds for all u' by the same argument.
  Then R_beta T1 T2 body u with Habs : lc (abs T1 body).
  Where do we get lc (abs T1 body) = lc_at 1 body? From R T2 (open body u) we get lc (open body u), and lc u (from R T1 u); then lc_open_inv gives lc_at 1 body. ✓.
  
  Note: for the "forall u'" version of H, we need the freshness x for each u' — x is fixed, chosen fresh once. ✓.
  
  Also we need the substitution lemma to hold for arbitrary u' (not lc). It's syntactic, fine.

- T_App: Gamma, t1, t2, T1, T2; IH1 : ... R (T1->T2) (subst_env sigma t1); IH2 : ... R T1 (subst_env sigma t2). Goal R T2 (subst_env sigma (tm_app t1 t2)) = R T2 (tm_app (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2)). Apply R (T1->T2) ...'s arrow condition to the second. ✓
- T_If: Gamma, t1, t2, t3, T; H1 : t1 : Bool; H2, H3 : T. IH1 : R Bool (subst_env sigma t1) = lc ∧ SN; IH2, IH3 : R T ... Goal R T (subst_env sigma (tm_if t1 t2 t3)) = R T (tm_if u1 u2 u3) where ui = subst_env sigma ti.
  Need: R T (if u1 u2 u3). Hmm, this is a new case! We need to show that R is closed under if-then-else when the condition is in R Bool and both branches in R T. Since R Bool = lc ∧ SN, u1 is SN (and lc). We can prove this by Acc induction on SN u1, similar to the beta case:
    - if u1 is a value: values at Bool... u1 is syntactically a value and R Bool. Values are abs/true/false. If u1 = true: if true u2 u3 --> u2 (ST_IfTrue, needs lc u2, lc u3 ✓ from R T). Unique reduct? ST_If requires u1 to step: true doesn't step. So unique. Then inverse closure at T gives R T (if true u2 u3) from R T u2. ✓
      If u1 = false: similar.
      If u1 = abs T0 t0: abs has type... syntactically a value of Bool type (the relation is untyped so R Bool (abs T0 t0) holds if lc abs ∧ SN abs). Then `if (abs ...) u2 u3` is stuck (ST_If requires step; value doesn't step; ST_IfTrue/False require t1 = true/false literal). Not a value (it's an if). Stuck lemma applies → R T (if abs u2 u3)? The stuck lemma requires lc (if...) (✓ lc u1 from R Bool, lc u2, u3), no steps ✓, ~value ✓. ✓
    - if u1 is not a value:
       * if u1 steps to u1': if u1 u2 u3 --> if u1' u2 u3 (ST_If, needs lc u2, lc u3 ✓). Unique reduct (ST_IfTrue/False need t1 = true/false: true/false are values, so if u1 steps, u1 ≠ true,false, and value u1 would be abs — but u1 not a value). So unique. Need R T (if u1' u2 u3): Acc IH on u1' with R Bool u1' (R_step_fwd) and R T u2/u3. ✓ Then inverse closure at T. ✓
       * if u1 doesn't step: stuck lemma: if u1 u2 u3 has no steps (ST_If requires step u1; true/false rules require u1 = true/false which are values, excluded; so ✓), lc ✓, ~value ✓ → R T. ✓
  Again a case split on value u1 and whether u1 steps. But just like R_beta, we can prove the reduct condition by Acc induction without deciding: For the term s = if u1 u2 u3, we can use K-style reasoning? `if` is not an application, so K_app doesn't apply directly. But we can prove a general "inverse closure" for terms whose one-step reducts are in R T, provided the term is not a value and ... hmm, the issue is again the arrow condition: if T = A->B, R T (if u1 u2 u3) requires forall w ∈ R A, R B ((if u1 u2 u3) w). The term is an application whose function is an if-term. K_app applies to it! Because (if ...) w is an application. And K_app's hypothesis requires all reducts of (if ...) w in R B; reducts: ST_App1 with if --> if' (lc w ✓) → (if') w, which is in R B *if* R T (if') holds... but we're in the middle of proving R T (if ...). Hmm, we need a coherent induction.

  Let's structure the if case as: prove by Acc induction on SN u1 that:
     (a) forall s', step (if u1 u2 u3) s' -> R T s'
     (b) SN (if u1 u2 u3)
     (c) R T (if u1 u2 u3)
  But (c) via K_app doesn't work because the if-term is not an application; at arrow type T we need forall w, R B ((if ...) w). We can prove R B ((if ...) w) using K_app at type B applied to the application `(if ...) w`, whose reduct condition requires: for any reduct of `(if ...) w`, it's in R B. Reducts: ST_App1 with (if ...) --> s' and lc w: (s') w. Need R B (s' w); from (a) R T s' (T = A->B), arrow condition gives R B (s' w) ✓. ST_AppAbs/ST_App2 impossible (function is an if-term, not a value/abs). ✓. And SN ((if ...) w): from the reduct condition via R_SN ✓. Then K_app gives R B ((if ...) w) ✓. So (c) is derivable from (a) [reduct condition] and the arrow condition of those reducts — without needing (c) itself! Nice: K_app at type B requires (a') all reducts of ((if...) w) in R B, which follows from (a). So:
  
  So the general pattern: if we prove (a) forall one-step reducts of s are in R T, and SN s, then:
    - at Bool: R = lc ∧ SN, done with lc ✓.
    - at Arrow A B: R needs forall w ∈ R A, R B (s w). Prove via K_app at B for the application s w, whose reducts are s' w (ST_App1) — in R B from (a); other rules impossible if s is not a value and not an abs. Wait: if s = abs, ST_AppAbs applies to s w, and (a) is vacuous; that's the abstraction case (needs H). For s = if ... (not a value, not an abs), ST_AppAbs doesn't apply to s w, but ST_App2 requires value s w? No, ST_App2 requires value s, which is false. ST_App1 requires lc w ✓ and step s s' — for those, (a) applies. So K_app works, provided we know SN (s w) and the reduct condition. SN (s w) follows from the reduct condition + R_SN (since each reduct is in R B hence SN). But to state K_app's hypothesis we need "forall f', step (s w) f' -> R B f'" which we have; SN (s w) is derivable from that by Acc_intro. Good.
    So actually we can prove a **general lemma**:

    `R_of_reducts : forall T s, locally_closed s -> (forall s', step s s' -> R T s') -> R T s`, provided s is not a value and not an abstraction? Let's check: if s is not a value and not abs (or more precisely, if s w only reduces via ST_App1 for all w), then the proof works. What if s is a value but not an abs: s = true/false. Then s w's reductions: ST_AppAbs no (s ≠ abs); ST_App2 requires value s = true ✓ and w steps: reduct s w'. Hmm! So `true w` can step via ST_App2 reducing w. So for s = true/false, the arrow condition requires handling ST_App2 reducts: R B (true w') with w --> w', which would need R B (true w) by induction on SN w. Hmm. So R_of_reducts doesn't hold for s = true at arrow type in general? But maybe true ∈ R (A->B) is still true (by a separate argument), but the reduct condition is vacuous, so R_of_reducts would wrongly conclude R (A->B) true without the needed application property. Is R (A->B) true actually true? For arbitrary A,B: need forall w ∈ R A, R B (true w). R B (true w): if B = Bool: lc ∧ SN; SN (true w): reducts from ST_App2 if w steps → true w' (SN by Acc on w), and ST_App1 requires true to step (no), ST_AppAbs no. So SN ✓. If B = Arrow C D: forall z ∈ R C, R D ((true w) z). (true w) z: the function `true w` is an app; ST_App1 requires step (true w) s' — possible if w steps! Then reduct (s') z where s' = true w'; R D of that... by induction. And ST_App2 requires value (true w) — no. ST_AppAbs no. So a nested induction on SN w would prove it. So R (A->B) true likely holds, but proving it requires more than the reduct condition. Since our `s` in the if-case is an if-term (not a value), we're fine. In the beta-expansion, s is an application (not a value). For the stuck lemma, s is not a value. So we can restrict the general lemma to `~ value s`:

    `R_of_reducts : forall T s, locally_closed s -> ~ value s -> (forall s', step s s' -> R T s') -> R T s`.
    Proof by induction on T? Let's check the arrow case: forall w ∈ R A, R B (s w). Use K_app at B for the application s w. K_app requires: lc (s w) ✓; SN (s w); reduct condition: forall f', step (s w) f' -> R B f'. Reducts of s w:
      - ST_AppAbs: requires s = abs → value s: contradiction.
      - ST_App1: s --> s' and lc w: reduct s' w. R T s' from hypothesis; T = A->B; arrow condition of R (A->B) s' gives R B (s' w) ✓.
      - ST_App2: requires value s: contradiction. ✓
    So all reducts in R B. Then SN (s w) via Acc_intro + R_SN ✓. Then K_app gives R B (s w) ✓.
    Wait, but we didn't need the induction hypothesis on T here! We used K_app which itself is proven by induction on T. So R_of_reducts can be proven using K_app, no induction needed on T... Let's verify the structure: K_app is proven by induction on T. R_of_reducts is proven for arbitrary T using K_app at T (for Bool, direct; for Arrow, K_app at B for s w). Actually for Bool, R_of_reducts is direct (lc, SN from reducts). For Arrow, we need R B (s w), and K_app at type B (proven by induction on B) provides it. Great, no circularity: K_app's proof uses only the hypothesis, and R_of_reducts uses K_app. But wait, K_app's proof at arrow uses K_app at B (smaller type) — good.
    
    Hmm, but R_of_reducts's lc requirement: lc (s w) from lc s and lc w ✓.
    
    Now, does R_of_reducts let us handle R_beta more simply? In R_beta, we have s = app (abs T1 t) u, which is an application (not a value). We prove the reduct condition by Acc induction on SN u: reducts are open t u (from H) and app (abs) u' (from IH). So R_of_reducts T2 s gives R T2 s. And SN isn't even needed as a hypothesis — it's derived. 
    
    Similarly, the if case: s = if u1 u2 u3, not a value. Reduct condition by Acc induction on SN u1: reducts are u2/u3 (ST_IfTrue/False, from R T u2/u3) or if u1' u2 u3 (ST_If with R T via IH). Then R_of_reducts T s. ✓.

    Wait, but R_of_reducts requires lc s: for the if-term, lc u1 (from R Bool u1), lc u2, lc u3 (from R T) ✓.

    Hmm, careful with ST_IfTrue: the rule has premises locally_closed t1 and locally_closed t2 where the term is `tm_if tm_true t2 t3`; the lc premises are on t2 and t3 (the branches) — we have them. ✓.

So now the whole architecture is:

- SN := Acc step.
- R : Fixpoint ty -> tm -> Prop with lc, SN, arrow application.
- R_SN, R_lc.
- step determinism, values don't step, abs doesn't step, lc preservation both directions.
- K_app: applications in R given lc, SN, reduct condition. (Proof by induction on T.)
- R_of_reducts: for ~value s, lc s, all one-step reducts in R T → R T s. (Uses K_app; no induction on T needed, but needs K_app at subterm types; actually let me double check the proof for Arrow: we need R B (s w) via K_app. K_app at B applied to the application `s w`: its reduct condition uses R T s' for reducts s' of s. ✓.)
- R_beta: R T1 u -> (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open t u')) -> lc (abs T1 t) -> R T2 (app (abs T1 t) u). (Acc induction on SN u; use R_of_reducts.)
- R_if: R Bool u1 -> R T u2 -> R T u3 -> lc?? -> R T (if u1 u2 u3). (Acc induction on SN u1, using R_of_reducts.)
- R_step_fwd: R T s -> step s s' -> R T s'. (Induction on T; uses ST_App1.)

In K_app: reducts to R B from R T f1's arrow condition. No inverse. R_step_fwd: forward. So maybe R_step_inv isn't needed at all! Great — determinism might not even be needed! Let me double check R_of_reducts: it requires ~value s; reduct condition; lc. For Arrow: K_app at B for the application `s w` requires all one-step reducts of s w in R B:
     - ST_App1 (s --> s', lc w): s' w; R T s' (hypothesis); arrow condition of R T s' (T=A->B) gives R B (s' w) ✓ — no inverse needed.
     - ST_AppAbs: s = abs: contradicts ~value s ✓.
     - ST_App2: value s: contradicts ✓.
   So no determinism needed. ✓.
   K_app's SN requirement: we derive SN (app a b) from the reduct condition + R_SN by Acc_intro. ✓.
   
   Hmm wait, K_app's hypothesis includes SN f. In R_of_reducts, we call K_app on `s w` and need SN (s w). We derive it as above. But K_app's proof itself: at arrow, we call IH_B on the application `(app a b) w` and need SN of that; derived from reduct condition + R_SN. ✓. Fine.

So determinism is not needed! Great simplification. We only need: values don't step, abs doesn't step (special case of values don't step), lc preservation forward and backward, and ST_App1/ST_App2/ST_If behavior on non-values.

Let me re-examine K_app's proof at arrow more carefully:
Goal: R (A->B) (app a b) = lc ∧ SN ∧ forall w, R A w -> R B (app (app a b) w).
- lc: hypothesis.
- SN: hypothesis.
- For w with Hw: apply IH_B (induction hypothesis at type B) to the application term (app (app a b) w) with:
   * lc: lc (app a b) ∧ lc w ✓.
   * SN (app (app a b) w): derive by Acc_intro: for each reduct y:

       - ST_AppAbs: requires (app a b) = abs T0 t0 — impossible (constructor disjointness).
       - ST_App2: requires value (app a b) — impossible (value constructors are abs/true/false).
     So SN ✓.

   Then IH_B gives R B (app (app a b) w). ✓.

But the lemma statement has other parameters (a, b); the induction principle will generalize them if needed. Now, R_of_reducts proof:
`R_of_reducts : forall T s, locally_closed s -> ~ value s -> (forall s', step s s' -> R T s') -> R T s`.
Proof by induction on T? Actually as noted, for Bool it's direct; for Arrow, use K_app at B. But the statement is by induction on T... Let me write:
```
Lemma R_of_reducts : forall T s,
  locally_closed s -> ~ value s ->
  (forall s', step s s' -> R T s') -> R T s.
Proof.
  induction T as [| A B IH_A IH_B]; intros s Hlc Hnv Hred.
  - (* Bool *) simpl. split; [exact Hlc | apply Acc_intro; intros s' Hs; apply R_SN with (T:=Ty_Bool); apply Hred; exact Hs].
    Hmm, R_SN (Ty_Bool) s' : R Ty_Bool s' -> SN s'. R Ty_Bool s' = lc ∧ SN; Hred Hs gives it. ✓.
  - (* Arrow A B *) simpl. split; [exact Hlc|]. split.
    + SN: Acc_intro; intros s' Hs; apply R_SN with (T:=Ty_Arrow A B); apply Hred; exact Hs.
    + intros w Hw. 
      apply (K_app B (tm_app s w) ... ) -- K_app states R B (tm_app a b) given ... with a = s, b = w.
      * lc (app s w): from Hlc, R_lc Hw.
      * SN (app s w): Acc_intro; intros y Hy; inversion Hy as [...]:
          - ST_AppAbs requires s = abs → value s contradicts Hnv.
          - ST_App1: step s s' and lc w → y = app s' w; R T s' = R (A->B) s' from Hred; arrow condition gives R B (app s' w); then R_SN gives Acc. ✓
          - ST_App2: value s contradicts.
      * forall y, step (app s w) y -> R B y: same inversion; ST_App1 → R B (app s' w) from R T s' arrow ✓; others impossible.
Qed.
```
Wait: in the Bool case, `R_SN (Ty_Bool) s'` requires R Ty_Bool s' which is `lc ∧ SN`; Hred gives R Ty_Bool s'. R_SN is proven how? R_SN T s : R T s -> SN s. For Bool, R Bool s = lc ∧ SN s, so second component ✓. Need R_SN defined by induction on T or just case analysis: `destruct T; simpl in *; tauto` maybe. Let's define R_SN by `intros T; induction T; simpl; intros s H; [exact (proj2 H) | exact (proj1 (proj2 H))]`. Fine.

- SN part of R_of_reducts Arrow: Acc_intro with R_SN of reducts from Hred. ✓.

Lemma R_beta : forall T1 T2 t u,

R_if:
```
Lemma R_if : forall T u1 u2 u3,
  R Ty_Bool u1 -> R T u2 -> R T u3 -> R T (tm_if u1 u2 u3).
Proof.
  intros T u1 u2 u3 H1 H2 H3.
  assert (Hacc : Acc step u1) by (apply R_SN in H1; exact H1).
  assert (main : forall u1, Acc step u1 -> R Ty_Bool u1 -> R T u2 -> R T u3 -> R T (tm_if u1 u2 u3)).
  { intros u1 Hacc. induction Hacc as [u1 Hstep IH]. intros HR1 HR2 HR3.
    apply R_of_reducts with (T := T) (s := tm_if u1 u2 u3).
    - (* lc *) simpl. split; [apply R_lc in HR1; exact | split; [R_lc HR2 | R_lc HR3]].
    - (* ~ value *) intro Hv; inversion Hv.
    - intros s' Hs. inversion Hs as [...]:
      + ST_IfTrue: u1 = tm_true, s' = t2 (the second branch). Need R T u2 = HR2. ✓ (note the constructor names: ST_IfTrue : forall t1 t2, lc t1 -> lc t2 -> tm_if tm_true t1 t2 --> t1.)
      + ST_IfFalse: s' = t3, HR3.
      + ST_If: step u1 u1', s' = if u1' t2 t3; apply IH: Hstep gives Acc u1'; R_step_fwd HR1 gives R Bool u1'; HR2, HR3. ✓
  }
  apply main; auto.
```
Careful with the `inversion Hs` case analysis and the exact instantiation of the if branches.

Now the fundamental theorem. Let's detail the substitution machinery.

Define:
```
Fixpoint subst_env (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_env sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst_env sigma t1) (subst_env sigma t2) (subst_env sigma t3)
  end.
```
Hmm, wait: for locally nameless, substituting fvars with terms under binders — the standard subst_env is exactly this (no shifting needed if terms are closed, but syntactically this is the definition we use). The substitution lemma:

`subst_env_open : forall sigma x u t,
   ~ In x (free_vars t) ->
   subst_env (update sigma x u) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst_env sigma t)`.

Hmm, note `open_rec k (fvar x) t` and `open_rec k u (subst_env sigma t)`: need the same k. Let me define free_vars (fv) : tm -> list atom:
```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | true/false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.
```
Then prove the lemma by induction on t (generalizing k and x, u). For the abs case: `open_rec k (fvar x) (tm_abs T t1) = tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) t1)`, and subst_env on that; RHS: subst_env sigma (tm_abs T t1) = tm_abs T (subst_env sigma t1); open_rec k u (that) = tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) u (subst_env sigma t1)). Then IH with k := S k. ✓. Freshness: ~ In x (fv (tm_abs T t1)) = ~ In x (fv t1). ✓.

Also need: subst_env respects "no free variable" identity: if t is closed (fv t = nil), then subst_env sigma t = t. Prove `subst_env_closed : forall sigma t, fv t = nil -> subst_env sigma t = t`? Hmm, actually for the final theorem we need: `subst_env sigma t = t` where t has no free variables. But do we know empty |-- t : T implies fv t = nil? We need a lemma: `has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, In x (fv t) -> Gamma x <> None` (free variables are in the context). Equivalently, if Gamma x = None for all x (empty context), then fv t = nil. Prove:
`has_type_fv : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, In x (fv t) -> exists S, Gamma x = Some S`.
By induction on the typing derivation. T_Var: fv (fvar x) = [x], x in → Gamma x = Some T ✓. So the final theorem:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t : T -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  assert (HR : R T t).
  { apply (fundamental empty t T Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x)). intros x S Hx. unfold empty in Hx. discriminate. }
  apply Acc_SN. apply R_SN in HR. exact HR.
Qed.
```
where Acc_SN : forall t, Acc step t -> strongly_normalizing t (proved via Acc_ind + SN_intro as discussed).

Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=

  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l')

Lemma in_max_list : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.

Now, the substitution lemma and the `open`/`open_rec` interaction. Let me write the lemma carefully:

```
Lemma subst_env_open_rec : forall t sigma x u k,
  ~ In x (fv t) ->
  subst_env (update sigma x u) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)
  = open_rec k u (subst_env sigma t).
```
Proof by induction on t; intros sigma x u k Hfresh; simpl; try reflexivity...
- tm_bvar i: LHS: open_rec k (fvar x) (bvar i) = if eqb k i then fvar x else bvar i; subst_env of that = if eqb k i then (update sigma x u) x = u else bvar i. RHS: subst_env sigma (bvar i) = bvar i; open_rec k u (bvar i) = if eqb k i then u else bvar i. Need: (update sigma x u) x = u, i.e., if eqb x x then u else sigma x = u ✓ (Nat.eqb_refl).
- tm_fvar y: LHS: open_rec k (fvar x) (fvar y) = fvar y; subst_env (update sigma x u) (fvar y) = update sigma x u y. RHS: subst_env sigma (fvar y) = sigma y; open_rec k u (sigma y) = sigma y (open doesn't change fvar). Need update sigma x u y = sigma y, i.e., if eqb x y then u else sigma y = sigma y. Since x ∉ fv (fvar y) means x ≠ y, eqb x y = false. ✓ Need: x ≠ y from ~ In x [y]. 
- app: IH twice.
- abs T t1: LHS: subst_env (update...) (abs T (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) t1)) = abs T (subst_env (update...) (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) t1)); IH with k := S k. RHS: open_rec k u (abs T (subst_env sigma t1)) = abs T (open_rec (S k) u (subst_env sigma t1)). ✓
- true/false: refl.
- if: IH three times.

```
Fixpoint has_type_ind' (P : context -> tm -> ty -> Prop)
  (HVar : ...) ... (HAbs : forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1,
      (forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) ->
      P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2)) ...
  (H : has_type Gamma t T) {struct H} : P Gamma t T :=
  match H with
  | T_Var Gamma x T Heq => HVar Gamma x T Heq
  | T_Abs L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hpre => HAbs L Gamma T1 T2 t1 (fun x Hx => has_type_ind' P ... (Hpre x Hx))
  ...
  end.
```

Actually, `Acc_intro : forall (A : Type) (R : relation A) (x : A), (forall y : A, R y x -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x`. So `Acc R x` means x is accessible: all predecessors y with R y x are accessible. Hmm, this is the standard Coq definition where Acc is about *predecessors*, i.e., well-foundedness means no infinite descending chains. For our SN, we want: t is SN iff all its *successors* t' (t --> t') are SN. That's the relation reversed: define `SN t := Acc (fun x y => step y x) t`? Then `Acc_inv : forall x y, Acc R x -> R y x -> Acc R y`, so with R = flip step: Acc (flip step) t -> flip step y t -> Acc (flip step) y, i.e., step t y -> ... ✓.

Alternatively, define our own inductive SN:
```
Inductive SN : tm -> Prop :=
  | SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t.
```
This is exactly the shape of the given `strongly_normalizing`! And the induction principle generated by Coq for such an inductive: does `induction H` give IH `forall t', step t t' -> P t'`? `SN_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t`

Let me check R_step_fwd's proof by induction on T:
```
Lemma R_step_fwd : forall T s s', R T s -> step s s' -> R T s'.
Proof.
  induction T as [| A B IHA IHB]; intros s s' HR Hstep.
  - (* Bool *) destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn _]]. simpl. split.
    + apply lc_step_fwd with (s:=s); auto.
    + (* SN s' *) inversion Hsn as [s0 Hpre]. subst. apply Hpre. exact Hstep.
      (* Actually destruct Hsn: SN_intro t Hpre -> Hpre s' Hstep *)
  - (* Arrow A B *) destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Happ]]. simpl. split; [lc_step_fwd|]. split.
    + inversion Hsn; subst; apply H1; auto.
    + intros u Hu. apply IHB with (s := tm_app s u).
      * apply Happ. exact Hu.
      * apply ST_App1; [exact Hstep | apply R_lc in Hu; exact Hu].
Qed.
```
Hmm, `simpl` on R: R is a Fixpoint; `simpl` should unfold. Note R's match is on T; with T = Ty_Bool, R Ty_Bool s reduces to lc s /\ SN s /\ True. `simpl` might simplify `True` conjunction: `lc s /\ SN s /\ True` — Coq's simpl may reduce `True` to... it keeps `/\ True`. Fine.

Actually, careful: the definitional unfolding of `R Ty_Bool s`. If I define R as a nested Fixpoint with the match returning a Prop, `simpl` should work. Let me write R in a slightly different form to make it easier:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  strongly_normalizing t /\
  (match T with
   | Ty_Bool => True
   | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u)
   end).
```
The recursive calls R T1 and R T2 are at structurally smaller T ✓.

Now K_app:
```
Lemma K_app : forall T a b,
  locally_closed (tm_app a b) ->
  strongly_normalizing (tm_app a b) ->
  (forall s', step (tm_app a b) s' -> R T s') ->
  R T (tm_app a b).

Lemma R_beta : forall T1 T2 t u,
  locally_closed (tm_abs T1 t) ->
  R T1 u ->
  (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open t u')) ->
  R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 t) u).

  intros T1 T2 t u Hlcabs Hu H.
  remember Hu as Hu0. (* maybe not needed *)
  assert (main : forall u, strongly_normalizing u ->
     R T1 u -> (forall u', R T1 u' -> R T2 (open t u')) -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 t) u)).
  { intros u Hsn. induction Hsn as [u Hstep IH]. intros HR H.
    apply R_of_reducts with (s := tm_app (tm_abs T1 t) u).
    - simpl. split; [exact Hlcabs | apply R_lc in HR; exact HR].
    - intro Hv; inversion Hv.
    - intros s' Hs. inversion Hs as [T0 t0 v Hlc0 Hv0 | t1 t1' t2 Hstep1 Hlc2 | v1 t2 t2' Hv1 Hstep2 | ...]; subst.
      + (* ST_AppAbs *) apply H. exact HR.
      + (* ST_App1 *) exfalso. apply (value_no_step (tm_abs T1 t)); [apply v_abs; exact Hlcabs | exact Hstep1].
      + (* ST_App2 *) apply IH; [exact Hstep2 | apply R_step_fwd with (s := u); assumption | exact H].
  }
  apply main; [apply R_SN in Hu; exact Hu | exact Hu | exact H].
Qed.
```

Coq's induction tactic automatically reverts hypotheses that depend on the induction variable (u) if needed? The `induction` tactic does "dependent induction" only with `dependent induction`; plain `induction` will revert things that depend on the variable being eliminated? For `induction Hsn` where Hsn's index is u, plain induction will substitute/revert appropriately: it generally requires the goal and context to be generalized over u; if HR depends on u and isn't reverted, Coq may fail with "cannot find the elimination scheme" or produce a weak IH. ```

Here after `intros u Hsn`, the goal is `R T1 u -> ... -> R T2 (...)`. `induction Hsn` will generalize u? Hmm. Actually the standard pattern: `intros u Hsn. induction Hsn as [u Hstep IH].` — Coq will replace u with the constructor's variable and the goal `R T1 u -> ...` becomes `R T1 u -> ...` for the new u; but the IH needs to be `forall u', step u u' -> R T1 u' -> ... -> R T2 (app (abs) u')`. Will Coq generate that? The induction principle for strongly_normalizing is `SN_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t`. Here P must be the goal motive with u. Since the goal after `intros u Hsn` is `R T1 u -> ...` — a function type; Coq's induction tactic will generalize the conclusion over u? It typically requires that the goal be of the form P u with P a predicate over the index; here the goal is `R T1 u -> ...` where u occurs; Coq can still abstract: P := fun u => R T1 u -> ... -> R T2 (...) and the IH is `forall t', step u t' -> P t'` = `forall t', step u t' -> R T1 t' -> ... ` ✓. ```coq
Definition env_ok (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x).

Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_ok Gamma sigma -> R T (subst_env sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty as [Gamma x T Heq
                | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hpre IHpre
                | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2
                | Gamma | Gamma
                | Gamma t1 t2 t3 T H1 IH1 H2 IH2 H3 IH3]; intros sigma Henv.
  - (* T_Var *) simpl. apply Henv. exact Heq.
  - (* T_Abs *)
    simpl.
    (* Goal: R (T1 -> T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst_env sigma t1)) *)
    (* choose fresh x *)
    destruct (fresh_two L (fv t1)) as [x [HxL Hxfv]].
    (* Define body *)
    set (body := subst_env sigma t1).
    (* Show H : forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open body u) *)
    assert (Hmain : forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (open body u)).
    { intros u Hu.
      (* apply IHpre with x HxL, environment update sigma x u *)
      specialize (IHpre x HxL (update sigma x u)).
      apply IHpre.
      intros y S Hy.
      (* env_ok (update Gamma x T1) (update sigma x u) *)
      unfold update. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
      + (* x = y *) apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst y. 
        (* Hy : update Gamma x T1 x = Some S → S = T1 *) 
        unfold update in Hy. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl in Hy. injection Hy as ->. 
        unfold update. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. exact Hu.
      + (* x ≠ y *)
        apply Nat.eqb_neq in E.
        unfold update in Hy. rewrite E in Hy. (* wait, update Gamma x T1 y = if eqb x y... *) 
        unfold update. rewrite E. apply Henv. exact Hy.
    }
```

So `update` can't be used for sigma : atom -> tm! I need a separate environment update function for term environments:
```
Definition env_update (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
